THIS CONTEMPT OF COURT CASE (CIVIL) HAVING BEEN FINALLY
HEARD ON 3-11-2020, ALONG WITH Con.Case(C).1176/2019 AND
CONNECTED CASES, THE COURT ON 06-11-2020 DELIVERED THE
FOLLOWING:

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:447:-
J U D G M E N T
[COC Nos.1176, 1922, 1951,1953, 1995, 1999, 2032, 2298, 2584,
2640/19, 91, 93, 97, 103, 178, 185, 210, 211, 355, 365, 541,
569, 617, 635, 642, 657, 662, 749, 828, 865, 897, 899, 900,
905, 921, 958, 1003, 1006, 1007, 1008, 1013, 1052, 1057, 1064,
1076, 1077, 1080, 1089, 1092, 1094, 1095, 1097, 1099, 1101,
1128, 1136, 1145, 1169, 1271, 1282, 1283, 1288, 1289, 1318,
1330, 1347, 1358, 1403, 1435, 1499,1529,1566, 1581, 1582,
1587, 1594, 1599, 1602, 1605, 1657 & 1673/2020]
Dated this the 6th day of November, 2020
Shaffique, J.
These contempt cases have been filed alleging non
compliance of directions issued by this Court in a batch of writ
petitions. By virtue of the judgment, about five hundred and ten
writ petitions came to be disposed of by a common judgment.
The petitioners in those cases, that too several in numbers, were
employees of various establishments covered by the Employees'
Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952. The
grievance projected by them was that the respondents had not
extended the provisions of the Employees Pension Scheme, 1995.
They also challenged the provisions of Employees' Pension
(Amendment) Scheme, 2014 by which there was a drastic
reduction of the pension payable to them. Some of the writ

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:448:-
petitioners challenged the validity of the amendment as well.
The Division Bench after considering the substantial contentions
urged on either side, held from paragraph 38 onwards as under:-
“38. As rightly contended by the counsel appearing
for the petitioners, the effect of the amendments to
the Pension Scheme is to create different classes of
pensioners on the basis of the date, 1.9.2014, the
date on which the amended Scheme came into
force. Consequently, there would be -
(i) employees who have exercised option under the
proviso to paragraph 11(3) of the 1995 Scheme and
continuing in service as on 1.9.2014;
(ii) employees who have not exercised their option
under the proviso to paragraph 11(3) of the 1995
Scheme, and continuing in service as on 1.9.2014;
(iii) employees who have retired prior to 1.9.2014
without exercising an option under paragraph 11(3)
of the 1995 Scheme;
(iv) employees who have retired prior to 1.9.2014
after exercising the option under paragraph 11(3) of
1995 Scheme.
The rationale in so classifying the employees
covered by the Pension Scheme on the basis of the
above date is not forthcoming. The object sought to
be achieved is stated to be prevention of depletion
of the Pension Fund, which cannot be accepted as a
justification to support the classification. Inasmuch
as the statutory scheme is to make the Pension
Fund enure to the benefit of the homogeneous class

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:449:-
of the totality of employees covered by the
Provident Fund, a further classification of the said
class by formulating a Scheme is ultra vires the
power available to the Central Government under
Sections 5 and 7 of the EPF Act. Therefore, it has to
be held that, the impugned amendments are
arbitrary, ultra vires the EPF Act and unsustainable.
For the foregoing reasons, the petitioners are
entitled to succeed. The writ petitions are all allowed
as follows:
i) The Employee's Pension (Amendment)
Scheme, 2014 brought into force by Notification No.
GSR. 609(E) dated 22.8.2014 evidenced by Ext.P8 in
W.P.(C) No. 13120 of 2015 is set aside;
ii) All consequential orders and proceedings issued
by the Provident Fund authorities/respondents on
the basis of the impugned amendments shall also
stand set aside.
iii) The various proceedings issued by the
Employees Provident Fund Organization declining to
grant opportunities to the petitioners to exercise a
joint option along with other employees to remit
contributions to the Employees Pension Scheme on
the basis of the actual salaries drawn by them are
set aside.
iv) The employees shall be entitled to exercise the
option stipulated by paragraph 26 of the EPF
Scheme without being restricted in doing so by the
insistence on a date.
v) There will be no order as to costs.”

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:450:-
2. The contention urged by the complainants is that there
is wilful default on the part of the respondents in not complying
with the directions in the judgment. Though the employees had
taken steps by exercising the option as stipulated in paragraph
26 of the EPF Scheme, no action was taken by the EPF
Organisation to provide the eligible pension as contemplated
under the Scheme.
3. On the other hand, learned counsel appearing for the
EPF Organisation would submit that though by the aforesaid
judgment, the provisions of the amended scheme has been set
aside, including the consequential orders and other proceedings,
in the absence of a positive direction to provide pension to the
writ petitioners, it cannot be stated that the respondents have
committed wilful contempt of the directions. It is further stated
that though the EPF Organization had challenged the matter
before the Apex Court and it came to be dismissed, a Review
Petition had been filed before the Apex Court, which is pending
consideration. That apart, Union of India has also challenged the
aforesaid judgment before the Apex Court and both the matters
are being listed for hearing.

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:451:-
4. It is also pointed out by the counsel appearing on
behalf of the respondents in various cases that the declaratory
order passed in the case requires substantial time as well as
funds to arrange payment of pension as per the scheme
presently available. It is contended that when there is deficiency
of fund with the Provident Fund Organization and on a case to
case adjudication of the claims, thousands of claims are to be
addressed, the petitioners cannot rush to the Court complaining
that there is wilful default. The judgment of this Court in
Cont.Case (C) No.498/2020 dated 4/8/2020 in WP(C) No.9676/
2017 is also brought to our notice wherein another Division Bench
of this Court having considered the respective claims held that
there is no wilful or deliberate attempt on the part of the
respondents to comply with the directions contained in the
judgment. Accordingly, the contempt case was dismissed.
5. On the other hand, learned counsel appearing for the
complainants would submit that though there was no positive
direction to the Provident Fund Department to provide pension, it
makes an obligation on the part of the respondent department to
provide the pension as per the law laid down by this Court. It is

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:452:-
further submitted that in some of the cases, pension had been
paid to the respective pensioners based on the judgment of this
Court and presently the Provident Fund Department is taking a
double standard and is delaying payment of higher pension to the
said pensioners. Learned counsel also placed reliance on the
judgment of the Apex Court in Mafatlal Group Staff
Association and Others v. Regional Commissioner,
Provident Fund and Others [(1994) 4 SCC 58]. In the
aforesaid judgment, the Apex Court observed that the Provident
Fund Scheme is conceived in the interest of the retired persons
and for their benefit and it should be put in practice. It is the
statutory duty of the organization to ensure that the benefits
flowing from such contributions are commensurate with the
pension that are being paid. The Apex Court in that case had
after considering the contention that the amount collected from
the employees are far more than the benefit provided to them
held that the survival benefit should be an amount equal to
return of contribution with a realistic rate of interest. Anyway this
judgment may not have any application to the factual aspects
involved in the case especially in the light of the findings of the

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:453:-
Division Bench in the judgment in which we are concerned.
Counsel for petitioners relied upon a Division Bench judgment of
this Court in C.K.N.Nair v. K.M.Chandy (1976 KLT 879). This
Court had occasion to consider whether contempt proceedings
can be initiated if there is failure to comply with the declaratory
order. That was a case in which the Court had issued a direction
to the State Government to relieve the petitioner from the post of
Director, Small Farmers' Development Agency to enable him to
take up duty as Principal of the Agricultural College and
appropriate directions were issued in that regard. However, the
State preferred an appeal and during the pendency of the appeal,
petitioner was permitted to appear for an interview and he took
up another assignment as Director of Research, Rubber Board
which is under the control of Central Government. The matter did
not end there. Disciplinary proceedings were taken against him.
He was found to have involved in misconduct and the
Government decided to dismiss him from service. Chairman of
the Rubber Board directed him to hand over charge to the
Chairman or Deputy Director. He challenged the aforesaid action
by filing OP No.5270/74. The Original petition was allowed and

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:454:-
direction was issued to recall the suspension made by the
Government and also the order of the Rubber Board relieving him
from the post of Director of Research. It was also declared that he
is entitled to all consequential benefits as the Government did
not have authority to recall the petitioner to Government service
or suspend him from service. It was clarified that he continues in
the position of Director of Research in the Rubber Board. Though
the petitioner requested the Chairman of Rubber Board to permit
him to carry on duties as Director of Research, he did not do so,
and after a few weeks, he filed a contempt case. The question
that came up for consideration before the Division Bench was
whether there was justification on the part of Rubber Board in
issuing proceedings dated 27/1/1976. It is while considering the
above matter that the Division Bench observed at paragraphs 13
and 14 as under:-
“13. The next aspect is to see whether the
respondent has committed any contempt. 'Civil
contempt' is defined in S.2(b) to mean 'wilful
disobedience to any judgment, decree, direction,
order, writ or other process of a court or wilful breach
of an undertaking given to a court ' Can it be said
that there was any wilful disobedience to the

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:455:-
judgment of this Court? This will depend upon the
nature of a declaratory judgment and the duty of
administrative bodies like the Rubber Board to
respect it A declaratory judgment is an order of the
court which declares what the legal rights of the
parties to the action are. Amnon Rubinstein in his
book 'Jurisdiction and Illegality', page 116, states the
principle thus:
 “ In the present context, a distinction ought to be
made between declarations seeking to prevent future
action (prospective declarations) and those
impugning past decisions (retrospective
declarations). Prospective declarations do not raise
any question as to the validity of decisions or
determinations; the court is merely asked to declare
that a certain course of action which is being
contemplated is illegal. The courts, in this case,
exercise a preventive supervision akin to that
administered through injunctions and prohibitions.”
 It is very often said that such a judgment has no
coercive force and therefore a disobedience of it will
be no contempt. But, it must be remembered that the
respondent represents a public body. A public body
cannot say that it is not prepared to observe the law.
In a country wedded to Rule of Law, obedience to
decisions of the Court by public bodies is implied by
its set up. It is rarely that a question is raised that a
public body is not prepared to observe the law or the
decision of a competent court. The effectiveness of a
declaration rests on the foundation that any public
body will always observe the law and the question of

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:456:-
disobeying the declaration will never arise. So a
coercive direction is unnecessary in the judgment.
Foulkes in his 'Introduction to Administrative Law',
Third Edition, page 183, states the principle thus:
 “But in many cases in administrative law (and
elsewhere) there is no question of the defendant,
often a public body, not being prepared to observe
the law: the problem is discovering what the law is,
rather than securing its observance. But though a
declaration has no coercive force it may effectively
undermine the enforceability of an administrative
act.”
Garner in his 'Administrative Law', Second Edition,
page 162, states the principle thus:
 “A declaration is subject to the defect that it is not
enforceable; in private law this is of course serious,
but in public law the defect is insignificant, as no
administrative agency can afford to be so
irresponsible as to ignore an adverse decision of a
High Court Judge.”
Again, in B. Mishra v. B. Dixit (AIR. 1972 S.C. 2466) at
page 2469 the principle is stated thus:
“Just as the disobedience to a specific order of the
Court undermines the authority and dignity of the
court in a particular case, similarly any deliberate and
mala fide conduct of not following the law laid down
in the previous decision undermines the
constitutional authority and respect of the High
Court. Indeed, while the former conduct has
repercussions on an individual case and on a limited
number of persons, the latter conduct has a much

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:457:-
wider and more disastrous impact. It is calculated not
only to undermine the constitutional authority and
respect of the High Court generally, but is also likely
to subvert the Rule of Law and engender harassing
uncertainty and confusion in the administration of
law.”
 In the light of these principles it is clear that there
is no great difference in substance between the
positive form of a declaration and the negative form
of an injunction so far as public bodies are concerned
and the Rubber Board can ill-afford to disregard the
legal effect of the declaration granted by this Court in
O. P. No. 5270 of 1974 that the petitioner is declared
to be continuing in the service of the Rubber Board
and that the legal termination of service can be only
as stated therein. The conduct of the respondent is
calculated not only to undermine the constitutional
authority of this Court but is also likely to subvert the
rule of law.
14 . The learned Advocate General submitted that in
order to constitute a civil contempt there must be
proof of a clear contumacious conduct which cannot
be explained otherwise. He also submitted that wilful
culpability should be established and if two views are
possible on the matter reasonably the benefit of
doubt be given to the person alleged to have
committed contempt and that all these are absent in
this case. In support of this he referred to the
decisions of the Supreme Court in Dahabrata v. State
(AIR. 1969 SC. 189), S.S. Roy v. State of Orissa (AIR.
1960 SC 190), Aligarh Municipality v. E.T. Mazdoor

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:458:-
Union (AIR. 1970 SC. 1767) and Banna Prasad v.
State of U. P. (AIR. 1968 SC. 1348). The expression
“wilful” in the definition of the word ''civil contempt”
is intended only to exclude such causal or accidental
or unintentional act. The facts stated previously
clearly make out that the conduct of the respondent
was such that no reasonable man in his position
would have thought that the order of appointment of
the petitioner ceased with 20-3-1975. Moreover, this
court clearly stated the method by which petitioner's
service can be declared as terminated. All these are
deliberately ignored. Therefore, we are satisfied that
action of the respondent in not permitting the
petitioner to resume his duties and in issuing the
proceedings dated 27 1 1976 is clearly a disrespect
shown to the constitutional authority of this Court
and thereby the respondent has committed
contempt.”
6. Learned counsel also placed reliance on the order of
the Apex Court in Phunindre Singh H. (Dr.) and Others v.
K.K.Sethi and Another [(1998) 8 SCC 640] by which the Apex
Court directed the contempt cases to be disposed of on merits
instead of adjourning the same till the disposal of the appeal.
7. There cannot be any controversy on the issues
involved in the matter. We have to consider the question
whether there is wilful contempt in the case. Apparently, this is a

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:459:-
case in which a declaratory direction had been issued by this
Court after setting aside the amendment, which came into force
as per notification dated 22/8/2014. All other consequential
orders were also set aside and finally this Court held that the
employees shall be entitled to exercise the option stipulated by
paragraph 26 of the EPF Scheme without being restricted in doing
so by the insistence on a date. The follow up measure that is
required in the matter is that, once the option is exercised, the
employees are entitled for pension as per the scheme. The
substantial contention urged on behalf of the complainants is that
the respondent organization, being a public authority, while
accepting the option, is bound to pay the pension. There is no
doubt about the fact that when the judgment is accepted and had
become final, there is no option available with the EPF
Organization other than to comply with the directions. When a
permission is granted to exercise an option as per paragraph 26
of the Scheme, consequently, the pensioners are entitled for a
pension as per the prevalent scheme. It is the duty and obligation
on the part of the EPF Organization to ensure that the pension is
paid after exercise of such an option.

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:460:-
8. True, as contended by the learned counsel for the
respondents, payment of pension requires substantial funds and
if the entire pension is paid as directed, there will be depreciation
of the source of funds which may ultimately result in closure of
the EPF organization itself.
9. In Contempt Case No.1094/2020, a counter affidavit
had been filed by the Regional Provident Fund Commissioner.
Paragraphs 13 to 16 reads as under:-
“13. By the judgment and order dated 12.10.2018, this
Hon'ble Court had struck down the amendments made to
the Employees' Pension Scheme 1995, (“the pension
scheme") by GSR 609 (E) dated 22.08.2014. The result of
this judgment was that the following changes brought to
the pension scheme were done away with.
a. The pension scheme provides for a pension to
employees on the basis of their pay; vide paragraph 3.
Before the 2014 amendment, pay up till Rs 6,500/- p.m.
was considered for the pension. The 2014 amendment
increased this ceiling to Rs. 15,000/-. This strengthened
the pension cover for all the employees who were
earning more than Rs. 6,500/-.The judgment of
12.10.2018 struck down this amendment and reduced
this ceiling back to Rs. 6,500/-. This adversely affected all
the employees who had benefited from the increase in
the ceiling.
b. Monthly pension payable to an employee is

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:461:-
determined under paragraph 12 on the basis of
pensionable salary and pensionable service. Pensionable
salary is defined under paragraph 11 as the average pay
drawn by an employee prior to his or her superannuation
from the pension scheme. Earlier, the pensionable salary
used to be the average pay drawn in the last 12 months
prior to superannuation from pension scheme. By the
2014 amendment, this duration was increased to last 60
months prior to superannuation from pension scheme.
This was done to get a truer picture of the pay drawn by
an employee towards his or her Superannuation from the
pension scheme. The judgment of 12.10.2018 struck
down this amendment and restored pensionable salary
as average pay drawn for last 12 months prior to
superannuation from pension scheme.
c. As stated in points (a) and (b) above, the pay ceiling
provided for in paragraph 3, applies to the pensionable
salary in paragraph 11 as pensionable salary is nothing
but average pay drawn towards the superannuation from
pension scheme. In the proviso to paragraph 11(3)
employees and employers had the option to submit a
joint request to EPFO to contribute to the pension fund
over and above the ceiling. The 2014 amendment
deleted this proviso and made the ceiling absolute.
However, it protected the interest of such employees
whose employers were contributing above the ceiling by
offering them an opportunity to submit fresh joint
request with their employer under paragraph 11(4). The
judgment dated 12.10.2018 struck down this amendment
and restored the proviso.
14. As a result of this judgment about 2.34 crores

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:462:-
employees who are members of pension scheme and
earning less than Rs. 15,000/- per month but more than
Rs. 6,500/- per month were adversely affected as the pay
ceiling was reduced to Rs. 6,500/-. Additionally, about 45
lakhs employees who had superannuated from the
pension scheme with vested rights to increased pension
under 2014 amendment were also adversely affected.
15. Apart from striking down the 2014 amendment,
the 12.10.2018 judgment permitted employees who had
superannuated from the pension scheme, to
retrospectively exercise the joint option under the
proviso to paragraph 11(3). This caused a huge financial
strain on the health of the pension fund. The pension
scheme is funded from contributions from employers,
contemporaneously to the payment of pay/salary every
month. Thereafter, the entire fund is subjected to an
annual actuarial analysis under paragraph 32. The
pension fund is planned by the actuary in this annual
exercise on the basis of the amounts that are received or
are receivables. This procedure of planning the health of
pension fund has been approved of and upheld by a
Three Judge Bench of Hon'ble Supreme Court in Mafatlal
Group Staff Association and others v. Regional
Commissioner Provident Fund and others (1994) 4 SCC
58. This decision was followed by a Two Judge Bench of
Hon'ble Supreme Court and Otis Elevators Employees'
Union Society Registered v. Union of India and others
(2003) 12 SCC 68.
16. By permitting retrospective contributions by
employees who are no longer members of the pension
fund, the judgment of 12.10.2018 forces EPFO to pay

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:463:-
pension on amounts that were never the subject matter
of the annual actuarial analyses conducted over several
years. Evidently, such retrospective contribution goes
contrary to paragraph 32 of the pension scheme and the
law laid down by Hon'ble Supreme Court in the two
Judgments mentioned above. As per the actuarial deficit
of Rs.8,73,263.75 Crores in case of establishments
exempted from EPF Scheme and Rs.6,55,255.72 Crores
in case of establishments un-exempted from EPF
Scheme, vide actuarial reports dated 2712-2018. The
explanatory statement dated 20-06-2019 is produced
herewith and marked as Annexure Annexure R-2(a)."
He also referred to order dated 6/2/2020 of the Apex Court, which
reads as under:-
“Learned Attorney General has pointed out to us that
the petitions arsing out from the judgment of the
Kerala High Court being SLP(Civil)Nos. 16721-
16722/2019 have been directed to be listed before a
Bench of Hon'ble the Chief Justice, Hon'ble Mr. Justice
Deepak Gupta and Hon'ble Mr.Justice Anirudda Bose
(Three-Judge Bench) in terms of order dated 12th July
2019
and even the Review Petitions have been directed to
be listed in open Court. We are of the view that
undoubtedly this would have ramification in the
present matters and thus it may be appropriate to list
the present matters also along with those matters so
that a call can be taken whether these matters have to
be heard after the decision of that matter or whether
the parties in the present matters will also get an

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:464:-
opportunity to address the Court in those
proceedings.'
Further it is stated at paragraph 21 as under:-
“21. In the meanwhile, several contempt petitions have
been submitted before this Hon'ble Court seeking
implementation of the judgment dated 12.10.2018.
EPFO has been forced to comply with these contempt
petitions despite the fact that Hon'ble Supreme Court is
considering the issue on merits. As of today, as many as
38 contempt petitions have been decided resulting in
the payment of Rs. 8.45 crores. In addition to the
above, 308 contempt petitions are still pending of which
Rs.53.81 crores in respect of 1205 petitioners have
been already been paid. Compliance with these
contempt petitions would cause an additional burden of
Rs.1137.37 crores.”
10. Going by the aforesaid details, we are of the view that
it is not proper to put such pressure on the officers of the EPF
organization when on account of the aforesaid judgment,
substantial amounts have to be given to the complainants.
According to them, the actuarial deposit would be `8,73,263.75
crores in the case of establishments exempted from EPF scheme
and `6,55,255.72 crores in the case of establishments which are
not exempted from the EPF Scheme. Apparently, the scheme
requires a substantial contribution and even if the pensioners do

Cont.Case (C) No.1176/2019 & conn.cases
-:465:-
exercise their option and pay the amounts received by them to
the fund, it may not be possible to complete the process within a
short time. Probably that was the reason why this Court also did
not fix any time-limit for payment of the pension and had only
directed exercise of option.
11. Taking into account the factual aspects involved in the
cases, we do not think that these are fit cases in which action can
be taken for wilful contempt. However, taking into consideration
the plight of the petitioners, to prevent them from running from
pillar to post, we grant the respondents 6 months' time to comply
with the directions issued by this Court, subject of course to any
direction that might be issued by the Apex Court in this regard.
The contempt cases are, therefore, closed.
Sd/-
A.M.SHAFFIQUE
JUDGE
Sd/-
GOPINATH P.
Rp True copy
 P.S. To Judge
JUDGE
